union
R\cap\bigcup_{j\in[(r-2)r+1,r(r-1)]}Q_{j}
, minus all edges of the bricks in
\mathcal{B}_{r-1}
which are edges of
R
but not edges of any vertical path, where
R=P_{r+2}
if
r
is odd and
R=P_{r+1}
otherwise.

Next, for each
i\in[2,r-2]
, let
T_{i}
be defined as the union of bricks in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
union
(P_{r+1}\cup P_{r})\cap\bigcup_{j\in[(i-1)r+1,ir]}Q_{j}
, minus all edges of the bricks in
\mathcal{B}_{i}
which are edges of
R
but not edges of any vertical path, where
R=P_{r+1}
if
i
is odd and
R=P_{r+2}
otherwise.

Note, that all previously defined
T_{i}
’s are pairwise disjoint.
Moreover, for each
i\in[2,r-2]
,
T_{i}
contains precisely
2r
degree-
1
vertices,
r
of them, say
t^{i}_{1},\ldots,t^{i}_{r}
ordered from left to right, are vertices of
P_{r}
, and we call them the
top endpoints of
T_{i}
, and
r
of them say
b^{i}_{1},\ldots,b^{i}_{r}
ordered from left to right, are vertices of
P_{r+1}
, and we call them the
bottom endpoints of
T_{i}
.
Additionally, there are precisely
r^{\prime}
degree-
1
vertices of
T_{1}
, say
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r^{\prime}}
ordered from left to right, and precisely
r
degree-
1
vertices of
T_{r-1}
,
say
y_{1},\ldots,y_{r^{\prime}}
ordered from left to right.

Our goal is to find a collection of linkages in
M
, internally disjoint from all
T_{i}
’s that join the
x_{i}
’s in left to right order to the
b^{2}_{j}
’s in the reverse order, then, for each
i\in[2,r-2]
, join the
t^{i}_{j}
’s in left to right order to the
t^{i+1}_{j}
’s in the reverse order, and join the
b^{i+1}_{j}
’s in left to right order to the
b^{i+2}_{j}
’s in reverse order, and so on, until we reach the
y_{i}
’s.

It follows that
M
provides the necessary infrastructure for this routing.
See
??
for an illustration on how to find these paths.
In fact, by construction, if we take the union of all
T_{i}
’s with the linkages we find, we obtain an
r
-mesh
M^{\prime}
such that moreover, for every brick
B
of
M
, the subgraph of
G
induced by
B
contains the subgraph of
G
induced by precisely one brick among those contained in a family
\mathcal{B}_{i}
above, and as a result
M^{\prime}
is red in
\rho
.
Also clearly,
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{M}
.
∎

Figure 8
:
An illustration for the proof of
??
for
r=4.

The second lemma extracts a red grid minor from a red, flat mesh.

Lemma 3.5
.

For every integer
k\geq 2
the following holds.
Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a
(3k-1)
-mesh
M\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho.

Then,
(G,R)
contains
\mathsf{R}_{k}
as a red minor.

Proof.

Let us denote the vertical paths of
M
by
P_{1},\ldots,P_{3k-1}
and the horizontal paths of
M
by
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{3k-1}
.
Let
M^{\prime}
denote the
2k
-submesh of
M
obtained from the union of the
P_{i}
’s and
Q_{i}
’s with indices in the set

\{3m+1,3m+2\mid m\in[0,k-1]\}.

Moreover, for every
i,j\in[0,k-1]
, denote by
B_{ij}
the brick of
M
that is the unique cycle in
P_{3i+1}\cup P_{3i+2}\cup Q_{3j+1}\cup Q_{3j+2}
and let
\mathcal{B}_{M^{\prime}}=\{B_{ij}\mid i,j\in[0,k-1]\}
.

By definition, for every
i,j\in[0,k-1]
, it follows that the subgraph
H_{B_{ij}}
of
G
induced by
B_{ij}
is connected, contains
B_{ij}
, and since
M
is red in
\rho
,
V(H_{B_{ij}})\cap R\neq\emptyset
.
Moreover, for distinct
B,B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{M}
,
H_{B}
is disjoint from
H_{B^{\prime}}
, and we ensure this by skipping over every 3rd horizontal and vertical path in the definition of
M^{\prime}
.

Now, it is easy to see that
M^{\prime}
contains a
\mathsf{R}_{k}
-minor-model where the branch set of each vertex contains precisely one of each
H_{B}
,
B\in\mathcal{B}_{M}.

∎

4
A society classification theorem for red minors

In this section we prove a variant of the society classification theorem for red minors with polynomial bounds.
This is the most technical and involved contribution of our work.

The society classification theorem with polynomial bounds.

We start from the society classification theorem for graphs.
The original comes from
[
38
]
.
Here we utilize the polynomial version due to
[
31
]
.

Proposition 4.1

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
31
]
)
.

There exist polynomial functions
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}},\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}},\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}
,
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
, such that for all integers
t,k,p\geq 1
the following holds.

Let
s\geq\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)
be an integer.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a cylindrical rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
around the vortex
c_{0}
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
for
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
.
Further, let
M
be the
s
-cylindrical mesh contained in
\bigcup(\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{R})
, and
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)}
-society in
\rho.

Then
G^{\prime}
contains a set
A\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
such that one of the following exists:

i)

A
K_{t}
-minor-model in
G
controlled by
M
.

ii)

A flat, isolated crosscap transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k))
around
c_{0}
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

iii)

A flat, isolated handle transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k))
around
c_{0}
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

iv)

A rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(G-A,\Omega)
in
\Delta
with breadth
b\in[\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(t-3)(t-4)-1]
and depth at most
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)
,
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)
, and an extended
k
-surface-wall
D
with signature
(0,0,b)
, such that
D
is grounded in
\rho^{\prime}
, the base cycles of
D
are the cycles
C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)-1-k},\dots,C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)-1}
, and there exists a bijection between the vortices
v
of
\rho^{\prime}
and the vortex segments
S_{v}
of
D
, where
v
is the unique vortex contained in the disk
\Delta_{S_{v}}
defined by the trace of the inner cycle of the nest of
S_{v}
, and
\Delta_{S_{v}}
is chosen to avoid the trace of the simple cycle of
D
.

In particular, the set
A
, the
K_{t}
-minor-model, the transaction
\mathcal{P}
, the rendition
\rho^{\prime}
, and the extended surface-wall
D
can each be found in time
\mathbf{poly}(t+s+p+k)\cdot|E(G)||V(G)|^{2}
.

In
[
31
]
, the following estimates for the functions in
??
are given.

\displaystyle\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}((t+k)^{9}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}(t^{8}),\ \ \mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t)\in\mathbf{O}(t^{3}),\ \ \mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k)\in\mathbf{O}(t+k),

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}((t+k+p)^{37}),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:societyclassification}}(t,k,p)\in\mathbf{O}((t+k+p)^{51}).

The annotated variant.

The goal of this section is to prove the following version of the society classification theorem for annotated graphs.

Theorem 4.2
.

There exist polynomial functions
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}},\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}},\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}
,
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{4}\to\mathbb{N}
, such that for all integers
r,k\geq 3
and
t,p\geq 1
the following holds.

Let
s\geq\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k).

Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a blank cylindrical rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
around the vortex
c_{0}
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
for
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
.
Further, let
M
be the
s
-cylindrical mesh contained in
\bigcup(\mathcal{C}\cup\mathcal{R})
, and
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be the
C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}}(r,t,k)
-society in
\rho
.

Then
G^{\prime}
contains a set
A\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
such the one of the following exists:

i)

A separation
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}
of order at most
t-1
such that
(Y\setminus X)\cap R=\emptyset
.

ii)

A red
K_{t}
-minor-model in
(G,R)
controlled by
M
.

iii)

A homogeneous, isolated crosscap transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k))
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

iv)

A homogeneous, isolated handle transaction
\mathcal{P}
of order
p
in
(G^{\prime}-A,\Omega^{\prime})
, with
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)
, and a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
of order
s-(\mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)+\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k))
to which
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal.

v)

A blank rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(G-A,\Omega)
in
\Delta
such either

•

there exists an
r
-mesh
M^{\prime}\subseteq G-A
that is grounded and red in
\rho^{\prime}
and such that
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{M}
, or

•

\rho^{\prime}
has breadth
b
at most
\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}(t-1)(3t-4)+r(r-1)-3
and depth at most
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k,p)
,
|A|\leq\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k,p)
, and an extended
k
-surface-wall
D
with signature
(0,0,b)
, such that
D
is grounded in
\rho^{\prime}
, the base cycles of
D
are the cycles
C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k)-1-k},\ldots,C_{s-\mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k)-1}
, and there exists a bijection between the vertices
v
of
\rho^{\prime}
and the vortex segments
S_{v}
of
D
, where
v
is the unique vortex contained in
\Delta_{S_{v}}
defined by the trace of the inner cycle of the nest of
S_{v}
, and
\Delta_{S_{v}}
is chosen to avoid the trace of the simple cycle of
D
.

In particular, each of these outcomes can be found in time
\mathbf{poly}(r+t+s+p+k)\cdot|E(G)|^{3}
.

Towards this, in the upcoming subsections we develop a series of tools that carefully combine ideas from the proof of
??
along with new techniques to deal with the red vertices, culminating in the proof of
??
in
??
.

At this stage, let us provide estimates for the function involved in
??
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}((r+t+k)^{144}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{genus}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}(t^{8}),\ \ \mathsf{loss}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(t)\in\mathbf{O}(t^{3}),\ \ \mathsf{cost}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k)\in\mathbf{O}((r+t+k)^{16}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{apex}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k,p)

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{O}((r+t+k+p)^{592}),\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathsf{depth}_{\ref{thm:blanksocietyclassification}}(r,t,k,p)\in\mathbf{O}((r+t+k+p)^{820}).

4.1
Dealing with flat crosscap or handle transactions

To prove
??
we eventually have to deal with outcomes ii) and iii) if
??
.
Our goal here is to first show that, similar to
??
, we may homogenize flat crosscap or handle transactions with respect to the red set.
Then, assuming the homogenized transaction is not blank, we show that using the mesh infrastructure provided by the union of the nest and the transaction, we may always find a large red grid minor in the graph.
This allows us to always conclude with a blank transaction.

Homogeneous transactions.

Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
(G,\Omega)
be a society.
Also, let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\}
be a naturally indexed, flat transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order
n\geq 2
and
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
be the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society in
(G,\Omega)
.

Let
X,Y
be the end segments of
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
such that
\Omega_{1}
is the concatenation of
X
and
Y
.
Moreover, for all
i\in[n-1]
, let
X_{i}\subseteq X
and
Y_{i}\subseteq Y
be the end segments of
\{P_{i},P_{i+1}\}
in
(G,\Omega)
.

Next, let
H\subseteq G
be obtained from the union of elements of
\mathcal{P}
by adding the elements of
V(\Omega)
as isolated vertices.
Given
i\in[n-1]
, consider any
H
-bridge of
G
with at least one attachment in

\big(V(X_{i})\cup V(Y_{i})\cup V(P_{i}\cup P_{i+1})\big)\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{n}),

and for each such
H
-bridge
B
let
B^{\prime}
denote the graph obtained from
B
by deleting all attachments that do not belong to
V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})\cup V(X)\cup V(Y)
.
We define the
i
-th
strip
of
\mathcal{P}
to be the graph
H_{i}\subseteq G_{1}
obtained from the union of
P_{i}\cup P_{i+1}
and all graphs
B^{\prime}
as above, after adding all vertices of
V(X_{i})
and
V(Y_{i})
as isolated vertices.
We also say that
H_{1}
and
H_{n-1}
are the
boundary strips
of
\mathcal{P}
.

Note that since
\mathcal{P}
is flat, and therefore
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
admits a vortex-free rendition in the disk, it follows that for every
H
-bridge
B
of
G
with at least one attachment in
V(\bigcup\{P_{2},\ldots,P_{n-1}\})\cup V(X)\cup V(Y)
,
B^{\prime}
defined as above is a subgraph of possibly both the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-th strip of
\mathcal{P}
for some
i\in[n-1]
, and is otherwise disjoint from any other strip.
Also note that
G_{1}=\bigcup_{i\in[n-1]}H_{i}
.

A flat transaction is called
red
if all its non-boundary strips contain a vertex of
R
or
blank
if no strip does.
In either case we call it
homogeneous
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all integers
p,q\geq 2
the following holds.
Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph,
(G,\Omega)
be a society, and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk.
Then for every flat transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
of order
qp
there exists either a blank transaction
\mathcal{Q}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
or a red transaction
\mathcal{Q}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
q
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{Q}
in time
\mathbf{O}(qp\cdot|E(G)|)
.

Proof.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{qp}
be an ordering of
\mathcal{P}
so that they are indexed naturally.
For all integers
i<j\in[qp-1]
, let
H_{i}^{j}=\bigcup_{l\in[i,j-1]}H_{l}
, where
H_{i}
denotes the
i
-th strip of
\mathcal{P}
.

For
i\in[q]
, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{(i-1)\cdot p+1},\ldots,P_{i\cdot p}\}
and let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
be defined by collecting the first path from every
\mathcal{P}_{i}
,
i\in[p]
.
Now we are in one of two cases.
Either there exists
i\in[q]
, such that
H_{(i-1)\cdot p+1}^{i\cdot p-1}
contains no vertex of
R
, or all do.
In the first case, we argue that
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is the desired blank transaction, while in the second case we argue that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is the desired red transaction.

It follows that any subtransaction
Q\subseteq\mathcal{P}
is flat.
Let
(G_{1},\Omega_{1})
be the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society in
(G,\Omega)
and
(G_{2},\Omega_{2})
be the
\mathcal{Q}
-strip society in
(G,\Omega)
.
To complete our proof it suffices to show that
G_{2}\subseteq G_{1}
and that for any two consecutive paths
P_{i},P_{j}
in
\mathcal{Q}
such that both
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
are non-boundary paths of
\mathcal{Q}
,
H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}^{j}
, where
H^{\prime}_{i}
is the strip of
\mathcal{Q}
that corresponds to
P_{i}
.

The fact that
G_{2}\subseteq G_{1}
follows by definition of strip societies and this already concludes the proof if we are in the first case above.
For the second property, let
X,Y
be the end segments of
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
so that
\Omega_{1}
is the concatenation of
X
.
Moreover, for all
i\in[n-1]
, let
X_{i}\subseteq X
and
Y_{i}\subseteq Y
be the end segments of
\{P_{i},P_{i+1}\}
in
(G,\Omega)
.

Let
H
denote the subgraph of
G
obtained from the union of elements of
\mathcal{P}
, by adding the elements of
V(\Omega)
as isolated vertices.
First note that strips, by definition, cannot contain isolated vertices which are not vertices of
V(\Omega)
.
Hence consider an edge
e\in E(H^{\prime}_{i})
.
If
e
is an edge of a path in
\{P_{i},\ldots,P_{j}\}
, then
e\in E(H_{i}^{j})
.
Moreover, by flatness of
\mathcal{Q}
,
e
cannot be an edge of a path in
\mathcal{P}\setminus\{P_{i},\ldots,P_{j}\}
either.
This implies, combined with the flatness of
\mathcal{P}
, that there is
l\in[i,j-1]
and
B^{\prime}
, where
B
is an
H
-bridge of
G
with at least one attachment in
(V(X_{l})\cup V(Y_{l})\cup V(P_{l}\cup P_{l+1}))\setminus V(P_{1}\cup P_{n})
, and
B^{\prime}
is obtained from
B
after deleting attachments that do not belong to
V(\bigcup\mathcal{P})\cup V(X)\cup V(Y)
, such that
e\in E(B^{\prime})
.
However by definition of strips for
\mathcal{P}
,
e\in E(H_{i})
, and therefore
e\in E(H_{i}^{j})
.
In a similar manner we may prove that
H_{i}^{j}\subseteq H^{\prime}_{i}
as well.
This concludes the proof for the latter case.
∎

Note that in case of a flat handle transaction
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{Q}
in
G
, where
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are its constituent transactions, we say that
\mathcal{P}
is
homogeneous
if both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{R}
are homogeneous in
G
.

Red, flat mesh or blank transaction.

We now guarantee that, given a cylindrical rendition of a society in the disk with a nest around a vortex and a large flat, red transaction that is orthogonal to the nest, we may always use the mesh infrastructure provided by the union of the nest and the transaction to find a mesh that is fully red.
This will allow us to clean up the crosscap or handle transactions implied by
??
for the proof of our local structure theorem,
??
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every integer
r\geq 2
the following holds.
Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a blank cylindrical rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
around a vortex
c_{0}
with a nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
r+2.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a red, exposed transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order
3r(r-1)-2
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and
\rho^{\prime}
is a vortex-free rendition of the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society in
(G,\Omega),
then there exists an
r
-mesh
M\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho^{\prime}
and such that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is a truncation of the tangle of a mesh whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from
\mathcal{C}.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
M
in time
\mathbf{poly}(r)\cdot|E(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
r^{\prime}=r(r-1).

Let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{3r^{\prime}-2}\}
be an ordering of
\mathcal{P}
that gives a natural indexing for
\mathcal{P},

\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{r+2}\},
and
\rho^{\prime}
be a vortex-free rendition in the disk
\Delta
of the
\mathcal{P}
-strip society
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
in
(G,\Omega).

Also, let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{3r^{\prime}-2}\}
where each
P^{\prime}_{i}
is defined as the unique maximal subpath of
P_{i}
with both endpoints in
V(C_{1})
and for
i\in[3r^{\prime}-1],
let
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
denote the two disjoint
V(P_{i})
-
V(P_{i+1})
-subpaths of
C_{1}
that are disjoint from all other paths in
\mathcal{P}.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\{P_{3m+1}\mid m\in\{0,\ldots,r^{\prime}-1\}\}.

Clearly
|\mathcal{Q}|=r^{\prime}.

Observe that since
\mathcal{Q}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and
|\mathcal{C}|=r+2,
we can find a
(2(r+1)\times r^{\prime})
-mesh
M\subseteq\bigcup(\mathcal{C}\setminus\{C_{1}\})\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
such that the unique subpath of every path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
contained in the intersection of
Q
with the crop of
G
by the
C_{r+2}
-disk in
\rho,
that intersects all cycles in
\mathcal{C},
is a vertical path of
M.

Clearly,
M\subseteq G^{\prime}
and grounded in
\rho^{\prime}.

We now claim that
M
satisfies the conditions to apply
??
that will give us the desired mesh.
What we have to show is that for every
m\in\{0,\ldots,r^{\prime}-2\},
the subgraph
H_{B_{m}}
of
G_{1}
induced by the brick
B_{m}
of
M
in
\rho^{\prime},
where
B_{m}
is the brick contained in the union of
P_{3m+1}\cup P_{3(m+1)+1}\cup C_{2},
contains a vertex of
R.

For this, it suffices to show the following.
Let
m^{\prime}=3m+2
and let
H_{m^{\prime}}
be the
m^{\prime}
-th strip of
\mathcal{P}.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is red,
V(H_{m^{\prime}})\cap R\neq\emptyset.

Further let
U_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq V(H_{n^{\prime}})
be defined as the vertex set of the union
P^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\cup P^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}\cup X_{m^{\prime}}\cup Y_{m^{\prime}}.

The first thing to observe is that the existence of
\rho
and the fact that it is blank, implies that every
(V(H_{m^{\prime}})\cap R)
-
V(M)
-path in
G
that is disjoint from
P^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}}\cup P^{\prime}_{m^{\prime}+1}
must intersect
C_{1}
precisely at a vertex of
X_{i}
or
Y_{i}.

This implies that any connected component
C_{m^{\prime}}
in the graph
H_{m^{\prime}}-U_{m^{\prime}}
which contains a vertex of
R
is disjoint from the connected component of
H_{m^{\prime}}-U_{m^{\prime}}
which contains
B_{m},
which in turn implies that
C_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq H_{B_{m}}.

Indeed, if this were not the case, then the existence of
\rho^{\prime}
would imply that there is a vertex
v\in V(C_{m^{\prime}})\setminus V(H_{B_{m}})
which
B_{m}
separates from
U_{m^{\prime}}
which is clearly absurd.

Moreover, by construction, it easily follows that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is the truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}
, where
M^{\prime}
is any
r
-submesh of
M
whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from
\mathcal{C}.

We may now call
??
to obtain an
r
-mesh
M^{\prime\prime}\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}
as desired.
∎

4.2
Towards a blank rendition

To prove
??
we eventually have to deal with outcome iv) of
??
.
The task here is to show that, starting from a bounded breadth and depth rendition of a society in a disk, one can either find a large flat, red mesh in the graph, or refine this rendition into a bounded breadth and depth rendition which is blank, i.e., red vertices are featured only within the vortices of this rendition.
In addition this has to be performed modestly in order to preserve polynomial functions.
This is the most technically challenging part of our proof.

4.2.1
Nest trees

In what follows we define an intermediate structure that will aid us in keeping track of the necessary structure to be subsequently refined in multiple steps over a series of lemmas that follow.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a rendition
\rho
in the disk.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a nest and
\mathcal{R}
be a radial linkage for
\mathcal{C}
.
Further, let
\mathfrak{C}
be a non-empty set of pairwise disjoint nests in
\rho
containing
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathfrak{R}
be a (possibly empty) set of linkages of
G
.

Further let
(T,r)
be a rooted subcubic tree and
\phi_{1}\colon V(T)\to\mathfrak{C}
,
\phi_{2}\colon E(T)\to\mathfrak{R}
be bijections satisfying the following properties.

Let
s_{0}\geq 1
be an integer.
First assume that for every non-leaf node
t\in V(T)
,
|\phi_{1}(t)|=s_{0}
, and if
\phi_{1}(t)=\{C^{t}_{1},\ldots,C^{t}_{s_{0}}\}
, let
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t}
denote the
C^{t}_{s_{0}}
-disk in
\rho
and
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{t}
denote the
C^{t}_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.

Moreover, assume that for every leaf node
l\in V(T)
,
|\phi_{1}(l)|\geq s_{0}+2
, and if
\phi_{1}(l)=\{C^{l}_{1},\ldots,C^{l}_{s}\}
, let
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{l}
denote the
C^{l}_{s}
-disk in
\rho
,
\Delta^{\mathsf{soc}}_{t}
denote the
C^{l}_{s-s_{0}}
, and
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{t}
denote the
C^{l}_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.

Further assume that,

T1

\phi_{1}(r)=\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{R}_{r}
is the
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{r}
-truncation in
\rho
of
\mathcal{R}
.
Then
\mathcal{R}_{r}
is an orthogonal radial linkage in the restriction of
\rho
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{r}
for
\mathcal{C}
.

T2

for every edge
tt^{\prime}\in E(T)
with
t
being the parent of
t^{\prime}
in
(T,r)
,
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t^{\prime}}\subseteq\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{t}
,

T3

if
t,t^{\prime}\in V(T)
are
siblings
, i.e., they have the same parent in
(T,r)
, then
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t}\cap\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.

T4

Moreover, for every edge
tt^{\prime}\in E(T)
with
t
being the parent of
t^{\prime}
in
(T,r)
,
\phi_{2}(tt^{\prime})
is a radial linkage in the restriction of
\rho
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t}
for
\phi_{1}(t)\cup\phi_{1}(t^{\prime})
that is orthogonal to
\phi_{1}(t)\cup\phi_{1}(t^{\prime})
.
Also, if
\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}
is the
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t^{\prime}}
-truncation in
\rho
of
\phi_{2}(tt^{\prime})
, then
\mathcal{R}_{t^{\prime}}
is a radial linkage in the restriction of
\rho
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t^{\prime}}
for
\phi_{1}(t^{\prime})
that is orthogonal to
\phi_{1}(t^{\prime})
.

T5

Further, if
t_{1},t_{2}\in V(T)
are siblings with parent
t\in V(T)
in
(T,r)
, then every path in
\phi_{2}(tt_{1})
is disjoint from every path in
\phi_{2}(tt_{2})
.
In particular,
\phi_{2}(tt_{1})
is disjoint from the crop of
G
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t_{2}}
and
\phi_{2}(tt_{2})
is disjoint from the crop of
G
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{t_{1}}
.

T6

Additionally, every vortex cell
c_{0}
of
\rho
is contained in
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}
, for some leaf node
l\in V(T)
.

We call the tuple
\mathfrak{T}=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},T,r,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
a
nest tree (in
\rho
)
of
(G,\Omega)
.

For an integer
t\geq 1
, we say that
\mathfrak{T}
has
linkage order

t
if
|\mathcal{R}|=t
and for every edge
e\in V(T)
,
|\phi_{2}(e)|=t
.
Moreover, for an integer
s\geq 1
, assume that for every leaf node
l\in V(T)
,
|\phi_{1}(l)|=s+s_{0}+1
.
We refer to
s
as the
cycle order
of
\mathfrak{T}
, while to
s_{0}
as the
reserve
.
We also refer to the number of leaf nodes in
T
as the
number of leaves
of
\mathfrak{T}
.

We refer to nodes of
T
as
nodes
of
\mathfrak{T}
.
We call
\mathcal{C}
the
root nest
of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
the
root radial linkage
of
\mathfrak{T}
.
For every node
t\in V(T)
we call
\mathcal{R}_{t}
the
truncated radial linkage for
t
(in
\mathfrak{T}
)
and the corresponding non-truncated version, the
radial linkage for
t
(in
\mathfrak{T}
).

For every leaf node
l\in V(T)
we call the set
\{C^{t}_{s+2},\ldots C^{t}_{s+s_{0}+1}\}
the
nest in reserve for
l
(in
\mathfrak{T}
).

Further, we call
C^{l}_{s+1}
the
boundary cycle for
l
(in
\mathfrak{T}
)
and
\phi_{1}(l)
minus both the nest in reserve and the boundary cycle for
l
, the
leaf nest for
l
(in
\mathfrak{T}
).

We also refer to the
\Delta^{\mathsf{soc}}_{l}
-society
(G_{l},\Omega_{l})
, for some leaf node
l\in V(T)
, as a
leaf society
of
\mathfrak{T}
, to the leaf nest for
l
as the
nest of
(G_{l},\Omega_{l})
,
and to the
\Delta^{\mathsf{soc}}_{l}
-truncation in
\rho
of
\mathcal{R}_{l}
as the
radial linkage of
(G_{l},\Omega_{l})
.

Given a set
R\subseteq V(G)
, we call a nest tree
\mathfrak{T}

R
-consistent
if every vertex of
R
is drawn in the interior of
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}
, for some leaf node
l\in V(T)
, and for every leaf node
l\in V(T)
, if
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}
contains no vortex cell of
\rho
, then there is at least one vertex of
R
drawn in the interior of
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}
.

Finally, given a second nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},T^{\prime},r^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi^{\prime}_{2})
in
(G,\Omega)
, we say that
\mathfrak{T}

respects

\mathfrak{T}
if the endpoints of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
on
V(\Omega)
are a subset of the endpoints of
\mathcal{R}
on
V(\Omega)
and
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
.

4.2.2
Developing the tools

Flat transactions in a fixed rendition.

Outcome iv) of
??
allows to assume that we are always dealing with societies for which we already know that there exists a rendition in a disk with bounded breadth and depth.
As it will become apparent later, in the absence of a large flat, red mesh, outcome iv) of
??
combined with
??
, will allows us to start from a society with a rendition in the disk of bounded breadth and depth and an initial nest tree with a single leaf.

Therefore, we may always assume to be working with such renditions which immediately allow us to deduce that any large enough transaction contains a still large subtransaction which is planar and resides in a vortex-free part of our rendition.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\}
be a naturally indexed planar transaction of order
n\geq 2
in
(G,\Omega)
.
Also, assuming that both
P_{1}
and
P_{n}
are grounded in
\rho
, let
T_{1}
and
T_{n}
be the trace of
P_{1}
and
P_{n}
in
\rho
respectively.
Then, there exists a unique open disk in
\Delta\setminus(T_{1}\cup T_{n})
whose closure
\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}
is bounded by both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
We call
\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}
the
container (in
\rho
)
of
\mathcal{P}
.

We say that
\mathcal{P}
is
\rho
-flat
if the restriction
\rho_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\rho
by
\Delta_{\mathcal{P}}
is vortex-free.

Note that
\rho
-flatness should not be confused with the flat transactions that were previously used.
This definition of
\rho
-flatness is used only in relation to a fixed rendition
\rho
.

Lemma 4.5
.

For all integers
b\geq 0
and
p,d\geq 2
the following holds.
If
\rho
is a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with breadth
b
and depth
d
, then every transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order
(b+1)(2bd+p)
contains a transaction of order
p
that is
\rho
-flat.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a transaction of order
(b+1)(2bd+p)
in
(G,\Omega)
,
I,J
be the end segments of
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
, and consider an ordering
P_{1},\ldots,P_{\ell}
of the paths in
\mathcal{P}
, ordered with respect to the occurrence of the endpoint
u_{i}
of
P_{i}
in
I
.

Suppose there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
such that there exists a path
P
in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
which crosses all other paths in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
.
If
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|>bd
, then there must exist a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order at least
d+1
and a vortex
c
of
\rho
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
induces a transaction of order at least
d+1
in the vortex society of
c
.
Since
c
is of depth at most
d
this is impossible and thus
|\mathcal{P}^{\prime}|\leq bd
.

We partition
I
into
b+1
consecutive segments
I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{b+1}
each containing at least
2bd+p
endpoints of members of
\mathcal{P}
.
For each
i\in[b+1]
there exists a family
\mathcal{P}_{i}
containing
p
consecutive paths such that
I_{i}
has at least
bd
other endpoints on either side.
Notice that, by the observation above, it is not possible that a path from
\mathcal{P}_{i}
crosses a path from
\mathcal{P}
which does not have an endpoint in
I_{i}
.
In particular, this means that for any choice of
i\neq j\in[b+1]
there does not exist a vortex of
\rho
which contains an edge of a path in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
and an edge of a path of
\mathcal{P}_{j}
.
Therefore, there must exist
i\in[b+1]
such that no path in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
has an edge or a vertex that belongs to a vortex of
\rho
.
It follows that
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is
\rho
-flat.
∎

Exposed transactions in non-cylindrical renditions.

Given a rendition of a society with a nest, in order to make any progress with our constructions, we require a large part of a large transaction in the society to traverse the interior of the inner cycle of the nest.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a rendition
\rho
of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots\}
.
A transaction
\mathcal{P}
in
(G,\Omega)
is said to be
\rho
-exposed
if the crop of
G
by the
C_{1}
-disk in
\rho
contains at least one edge of every path in
\mathcal{P}
which is not an edge of
C_{1}
.

Please note that the definition of exposed we use here differs from the one we previously used for cylindrical renditions.
The reason behind this is that for the proofs that follow we do not require the presence of an actual vortex cell for a transaction to be exposed.
For technical reasons, it simply suffices that the transaction crosses through the disk of the inner cycle of the nest.

Tighter nest trees.

As we have discussed, our target is to iteratively refine the initial single leaf nest tree.
There are two ways in which this refinement works.
Given a nest tree, we are either able to utilize a large exposed transaction we find in the leaf society of one of the leaf nests in order to split it into two new nests which will be placed as children to the first, which always increases the number of leaf segments by one, or in case this fails, we are able to find a “tighter” leaf nest that reduces the part of the graph that belongs to the corresponding leaf society.
This second trick will is in fact what allows us to find exposed transactions in the first place.
This trick originates from a technique used in
[
63
]
.

Let
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk and
\mathfrak{T}=(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R},T,r,\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
be a nest tree of
(G,\Omega)
.
We say that a nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{C}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}^{\prime},T,r,\phi^{\prime}_{1},\phi^{\prime}_{2})
of
(G,\Omega)
is
tighter (in
\rho
)
than
\mathfrak{T}
if
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
have the same cycle and linkage order, the same reserve, as well as the same number of leaves.
Moreover, they are identical, except for a unique leaf node
l\in V(T)
, for which the following holds.

Let
\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s+1}\}
be the leaf nest for
l
including the boundary cycle for
l
and
\{C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{s+1}\}
be the leaf nest for
l^{\prime}
including the boundary cycle for
l^{\prime}.

Then, there exists
i\in[s+1]
such that, if
H_{i}
is the crop of
G
by the
C_{i}
-disk in
\rho
and
H^{\prime}_{i}
is the crop of
G
by the
C^{\prime}_{i}
-disk in
\rho
, then either
H^{\prime}_{i}-V(C^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneq H_{i}-V(C_{i})
or
E(H^{\prime}_{i})\subsetneq E(H_{i}).

In case
i=s+1
above, we say that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is
strictly
tighter than
\mathfrak{T}.

Note that
\mathfrak{T^{\prime}}
clearly respects
\mathfrak{T}.

Finding a tighter nest tree is essentially accomplished by either slightly pushing one of the cycles of a leaf nest closer to the untamed area or by extending the nest into the untamed area.
To achieve this, we introduce the following supporting definitions.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk
\Delta
and let
\Delta_{0}
be a
\rho
-aligned disk in
\Delta
.
Also, let
T
be a closed curve in
\Delta
, or a curve with both endpoints in the boundary of
\Delta
.
Then, if one of the two regions of
\Delta-T
is a disk whose closure
\Delta^{\prime}
contains
\Delta_{0}
, we call
\Delta^{\prime}
the
\Delta_{0}
-disk
of
T
.
For a grounded subgraph
H
of
G
containing a cycle that possesses a
\Delta_{0}
-disk, the inclusion-wise minimal
\Delta_{0}
-disk of any cycle in
H
is the
\Delta_{0}
-disk
of
H
.

If
T
is the trace of a grounded cycle or a grounded
V(\Omega)
-path
C
and
T
has a
\Delta_{0}
-disk
\Delta^{\prime}
, then we say that
\Delta^{\prime}
is the
\Delta_{0}
-disk of
C
(not to be confused with the
C
-disk in
\rho
).

Now, given a grounded
C
-path
P
, we say that
P

sticks out towards
\Delta_{0}
(in
\rho
)
if the
\Delta_{0}
-disk of
P\cup C
is not the
\Delta_{0}
-disk of
C
and otherwise we say that
P

sticks out away from
\Delta_{0}
(in
\rho
).

With this we are ready to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.6
.

For all integers
s,p\geq 1
the following holds.
Let
(G,Z)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
with a
Z
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}
of cycle order
s
.
Moreover, let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be a leaf society of
\mathfrak{T}
and
\rho^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\rho
to
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
If
\mathcal{P}
is a
\rho^{\prime}
-flat transaction in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of order
2s+p+2
, then there exists either

•

a
\rho^{\prime}
-exposed,
\rho^{\prime}
-flat transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
, or

•

a tighter
Z
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
of
(G,\Omega)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathbf{poly}(s+p)\cdot|E(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
l
be the leaf node of
\mathfrak{T}
corresponding to
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
Let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s+1}\}
be the leaf nest for
l
including the boundary cycle for
l
and
\mathcal{R}
be the radial linkage for
l
.
Moreover, let
\Delta
be the
C_{s+1}
-disk in
\rho
in which the rendition
\rho^{\prime}
lives in.

If
\mathcal{P}
contains a
\rho^{\prime}
-exposed transaction of order
p
we are immediately done.
Moreover, we can check in linear time for the existence of such a transaction by checking for each path in
\mathcal{P}
individually if it is
\rho^{\prime}
-exposed.
Hence, we may assume that there exists a linkage
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
2(s+1)+1
such that
no
path of
\mathcal{Q}
is
\rho^{\prime}
-exposed.

Let
\Delta^{*}
denote the
C_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.
It follows that no path in
\mathcal{Q}
intersects the interior of
\Delta^{*}
.
Now, for each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
, let us call the disk defined as the closure of the region of
\Delta
minus the
\Delta^{*}
-disk of
Q
, the
small side
of
Q
.
Given two members of
\mathcal{Q}
, then either the small side of one is contained in the small side of the other, or their small sides are disjoint.
It is straightforward to see that saying two members are
equivalent
if their small sides intersect, defines an equivalence relation on
\mathcal{Q}
.
Moreover, there are exactly two equivalence classes, one of which, call it
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, contains at least
s+2
members.
Since
|\mathcal{C}|=s+1
there must exist some
i\in[2,s+1]
and a subpath
L
of a path in
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
such that
L
is a
C_{i}
-path that sticks out towards
\Delta^{*}
and
V(L)\cap V(C_{j})=\emptyset
,
j\in[s+1]\setminus\{i\}
.

Notice that
C_{i}\cup L
contains a unique cycle
C^{\prime}
different from
C_{i}
whose trace separates
\Delta^{*}
from the nodes corresponding to
V(\Omega)
.
Moreover, the
C^{\prime}
-disk
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
in
\rho
is properly contained in the
C_{i}
-disk in
\rho
.
In particular, there exists an edge of
C_{i}
which is not an edge of
C^{\prime}
and an edge of
C^{\prime}
which is not an edge of
C_{i}
.
Therefore, the crop of
G
by
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C^{\prime}
is properly contained in the crop of
G
by
\Delta^{\prime}
after deleting the vertices of
C_{i}
.

We would now like to define the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by replacing the edited cycle with
C^{\prime}
.
First, we are going to change the cycle
C^{\prime}
once more.
Consider
C^{\prime\prime}
to be the cycle obtained from
C^{\prime}
after iteratively replacing a subpath of
C^{\prime}
, in the same way as above, with any
C^{\prime}
-subpath of a path in
\mathcal{R}
that sticks out towards
\Delta^{*}
.
This step guarantees that the drawing of any
C^{\prime\prime}
-subpath of a path in
\mathcal{R}
is now a path that sticks out away from
\Delta^{*}
.
Call this property *.

The next and final step is to edit the paths in the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
in order to obtain a new radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
that is orthogonal to the new nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
obtained by replacing
C^{\prime}
with
C^{\prime\prime}
.
First, observe that the paths in
\mathcal{R}
may not be orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
only because they are not orthogonal to
C^{\prime\prime}
.
Now, for each path
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
R^{\prime}
be any
C^{\prime\prime}
-subpath of
R
and
C^{\prime\prime}_{1}
be the unique subpath of
C^{\prime\prime}
with the same endpoints as
R^{\prime}
such that the
(R^{\prime}\cup C^{\prime\prime}_{1})
-disk
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
in
\rho
avoids
\Delta^{*}
.
By property *,
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
does not intersect the interior of the
\Delta^{*}
-disk of
C^{\prime\prime}
.
In fact,
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
cannot intersect the drawing of any other path in
\mathcal{R}
, as this would imply the existence of a
C^{\prime\prime}
-subpath of a path in
\mathcal{R}
that sticks out towards
\Delta^{*}
which cannot exist.
Therefore, we may safely update
R
by replacing
R^{\prime}
by
C^{\prime\prime}_{1}
.
By repeating this procedure until we no longer find any such subpath
R^{\prime}
, we conclude with a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
orthogonal to
\mathcal{C^{\prime\prime}}
.

In fact, since all changes to
\mathcal{R}
happen locally within
\Delta
, by replacing
\mathcal{R}
with
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
and the nest
\mathcal{C}
with the nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathfrak{T}
, we obtain a tighter nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.
If moreover
\mathfrak{T}
is
Z
-consistent, then
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is clearly
Z
-consistent as the cycle
C_{1}
does not change.
∎

Orthogonalizing transactions and radial linkages.

What follows is a lemma that allows us to orthogonalize a transaction with respect to a nest in a given rendition of a society.
Before that we need to introduce the notion of a
cozy nest
in a society.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk
\Delta
with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Let
\Delta_{1}
be the
C_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.

We say that
\mathcal{C}
is
cozy
if for every
i\in[s]
and every grounded
C_{i}
-path
P
that sticks out away from
\Delta_{1}
we have
V(P)\cap V(\Omega)\neq\emptyset
or there exists a
j\in[s]\setminus\{i\}
such that
V(P)\cap V(C_{j})\neq\emptyset
.

The following lemma states that we may always turn a nest into a cozy nest.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
31
]
, Lemma 8.4)
.

Let
s\geq 1
be an integer,
(G,\Omega)
be a society, and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Then there exists a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of order
s
in
(G,\Omega)
such that the union of
C_{1}
and the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
contains
\bigcup\mathcal{C}^{\prime}.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}
in time
\mathbf{O}(s\cdot|E(G)|^{2}).

Next, whenever we swap a nest for a cozy nest we need to make sure to update any orthogonal radial linkage to the previous nest to an orthogonal radial linkage in the new nest.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society, let
\rho
be a
\Sigma
-rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in a disk
\Delta
, and let
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
be two linkages of the same order.
If for each path
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
there exists a path
P\in\mathcal{P}
with the same endpoints as
Q
, we say that
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
are
end-identical
.

The next lemma will allow us to do just that.

Proposition 4.8

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
31
]
)
.

Let
s,r
with
r\leq s
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
\Sigma
-rendition
\rho
with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and end-identical to
\mathcal{R}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm running in
\mathbf{O}(r\cdot|E(G)|)
-time that finds
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Now we can present the lemma that does the orthogonalization.

Proposition 4.9

(Choi et al.
[
6
]
(see also Lemma 4.16 in
[
49
]
))
.

Let
s,p
be positive integers.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a cozy nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\},
and let
H
be the outer graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho.

Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be an exposed transaction of order
s(p+1)+1
in
(G,\Omega)
with the end segments
X_{1},X_{2},
such that there exists a vortex-free rendition
\rho^{\prime}
of
(H\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P},\Omega)
that agrees with
\rho
on
H.

Then there exists a transaction
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of order
p
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and exposed in
\rho
,

ii)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
connects vertices of
X_{1}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
to vertices of
X_{2}\cap V(\mathcal{P})
, and

iii)

the intersection of
\bigcup\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with the inner graph of
C_{1}
in
\rho
is fully contained in
C_{1}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{P}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{O}(p\cdot|E(G)|)
.

We also need the following lemma that allows us to connect connect two radial linkages in a given society with a nest.

Proposition 4.10

(Gorsky, Seweryn, and Wiederrecht
[
31
]
)
.

Let
s,r,k,\ell
be positive integers with
s\geq r+3
.
Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society with a
\Sigma
-rendition
\rho
and a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Moreover, let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{R}
each be radial linkages of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that both are orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and let
I=[\ell,k]\subseteq[2,s]
be an interval with
|I|=r+2
.

Then there exists a radial linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order
r
in
(G,\Omega)
such that

i)

\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal to
\{C_{i}~\!\colon\!~i\in[s]\setminus I\}
with endpoints on
C_{1}
,

ii)

H_{\ell}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{\ell}\cap\mathcal{L}
, where
H_{\ell}
is the inner graph of
C_{\ell}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(C_{1})
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
on
V(C_{1})
, and

iii)

H_{k}\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}
is a subgraph of
H_{k}\cap\mathcal{R}
, where
H_{k}
is the outer graph of
C_{k}
in
\rho
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{P}
on
V(\Omega)
coincide with the endpoints of
\mathcal{R}
on
V(\Omega)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{P}
in time
\mathbf{O}(r|E(G)|)
.

We need one more tool that allows us to orthogonalize a radial linkage with respect to some nest in a respectful way.
This is essentially done by iterating the ideas in the proof of
??
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For all integers
s,r\geq 1
the following holds.
Let
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
with a nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
s
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}
.
Then there exists a nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of order
s
and a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
of order
r
for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and with the same endpoints on
V(\Omega)
as
\mathcal{R}
.
Further, the disk of the outer cycle of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in
\rho
is contained in the disk of the outer cycle of
\mathcal{C}
in
\rho
, and if
Z\subseteq V(G)
such that
\mathcal{C}
is
Z
-consistent, then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is
Z
-consistent.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
in time
\mathbf{poly}(s+r)\cdot|E(G)|^{2}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
Moreover, for each
i\in[s]
, let
\Delta_{i}
be the
C_{i}
-disk in
\rho
.

We define the desired nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,C^{\prime}_{s}\}
by iteratively applying the following rule: As long as there exists a path
R\in\mathcal{R}
, some
i\in[s]
, and a
C_{i}
-subpath
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
that sticks out towards
\Delta_{1}
satisfying
V(R^{\prime})\cap V(C_{j})=\emptyset
, for all
j\in[s]\setminus\{i\}
, we update
C_{i}
to the unique cycle
C^{\prime}
contained in
C_{i}\cup R^{\prime}
different from
C_{i}
, whose trace in
\rho
separates
\Delta_{1}
from the nodes of
\rho
corresponding to
V(\Omega)
.
Now observe that the
C^{\prime}
-disk in
\rho
is properly contained in
\Delta_{i}
.
Also notice that
C^{\prime}_{1}=C_{1}
as since
\mathcal{R}
is a radial linkage, there are no
C_{1}
-subpaths of any path in
\mathcal{R}
.
In fact this implies that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
remains
Z
-consistent.
This step can be performed in time
\mathbf{poly}(s+r)\cdot|E(G)|^{2}
.

Now, for
i\in[s]
, let
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
be the
C^{\prime}_{i}
-disk in
\rho
accordingly.
It follows from the previous step that any
C^{\prime}_{i}
-subpath
R^{\prime}
of any path
R\in\mathcal{R}
for
i\in[2,s]
, must either intersect
C^{\prime}_{i-1}
or it cannot be drawn
\Delta^{\prime}_{i}
.
From now on we call such a subpath
R^{\prime}
of a path
R\in\mathcal{R}
a,
regression
of
R
.

Let the paths in
\mathcal{R}=\{R_{1},\ldots,R_{r}\}
be ordered according to the occurrence of their endpoints on
\Omega
.
Moreover, assume there exists an
i\in[r]
and a
j\in[2,s]
such that
R_{i}
has a regression
R^{\prime}
with both endpoints on
C^{\prime}_{j}
.
Notice that there exists a subpath
L_{R^{\prime}}
of
C^{\prime}_{j}
that shares its endpoints with
R^{\prime}
which is internally disjoint from
R_{i}
.
This is because otherwise, we could find a subpath of
R_{i}
which witnesses that we are in the previous case.
Let
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
be the
(R^{\prime}\cup L_{R^{\prime}})
-disk in
\rho
which avoids
\Delta_{1}
.
We assume that
R^{\prime}
is chosen maximally with the property of being a regression.

Suppose there exists some path
Q
, possibly
Q=R
, in
\mathcal{R}
whose drawing intersects the interior of
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
in a node or arc distinct from those of
R^{\prime}
.
Then, this intersection of
Q
with
\Delta_{R^{\prime}}
must belong to a maximal regression
Q^{\prime}
of
Q
and there must exist
j^{\prime}\in[2,s]
,
j^{\prime}<j
, such that
Q^{\prime}
is a
C_{j^{\prime}}
-path.
Hence, there must exist some maximal regression
P^{\prime}
of some path
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
such that no other part of
\mathcal{R}
intersects the corresponding disk
\Delta_{P}^{\prime}
.
Thus, by replacing
P^{\prime}
with
L_{P^{\prime}}
, we obtain a new radial linkage with the same endpoints in
V(\Omega)
as
\mathcal{R}
but with strictly less regressions.
This means that, after at most
E(\mathcal{R})
many such steps, we must have found a radial linkage
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
which is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
This step can be performed in time
\mathbf{poly}(r)\cdot|E(G)|
.
∎

Blank transactions in a fixed rendition.

We now know that we may always tighten a given nest tree, or find within a leaf society, a large flat planar transaction which is also exposed.
Moreover, subject to making the nest of the leaf society cozy, we can use
??
to make this transaction orthogonal to the nest.
Similarly to
??
, we now want to use the mesh infrastructure provided by this transaction union the nest, to either find a large red mesh, or conclude with a large subtransaction that avoids all red vertices.

Let
(G,\Omega)
be a society and
\rho
be a rendition of
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a nest
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{s}\}
.
In a slight abuse of terminology, given a set
R\subseteq V(G)
, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is
R
-consistent (in
\rho
)
if every vertex of
R
is a vertex of the crop of
G
by the
C_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.

Moreover, let
\mathcal{P}
be a
\rho
-flat transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order at least
2
.
We say that
\mathcal{P}
is
R
-blank (in
\rho
)
if
\sigma(c)\cap R=\emptyset
, for every cell
c\in C(\rho)
that is either contained in the
\rho
-container of
\mathcal{P}
or for which
\sigma(c)
contains an edge of a boundary path of
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 4.12
.

For all integers
r\geq 3
and
p\geq 2
the following holds.
Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with an
R
-consistent nest
\mathcal{C}
of order
r+1.

If
\mathcal{P}
is a
\rho
-flat,
\rho
-exposed transaction in
(G,\Omega)
of order
r(r-1)p
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
, then there exists either

•

an
r
-mesh
M\subseteq G
grounded and red in
\rho
such that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is a truncation of the tangle induced by a mesh whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from
\mathcal{C}
, or

•

an
R
-blank transaction
\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
of order
p
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathbf{poly}(k+p)\cdot|E(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
P_{1}\ldots,P_{r(r-1)p}
be an ordering of
\mathcal{P}
so that they are indexed naturally and let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{r+1}\}
.
Moreover, let
\Delta^{*}
be the
C_{1}
-disk in
\rho
.
Also, let
P^{\prime}_{i}
be a
C_{1}
-subpath of
P_{i}\in\mathcal{P}
that is grounded in
\rho
and
T^{\prime}_{i}
be the trace of
P^{\prime}_{i}
in
\rho
.
Note that since
\mathcal{P}
is
\rho
-exposed and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
, each
P^{\prime}_{i}
is uniquely defined.

Now, for all integers
i<j\in[r(r-1)]
, there is a unique disk in
\Delta^{*}\setminus(T^{\prime}_{i}\cup T^{\prime}_{j})
, whose closure
\Delta_{i}^{j}
is a
\rho
-aligned disk bounded by both
T^{\prime}_{i}
and
T^{\prime}_{j}
.
Let
H_{i}^{j}
denote the crop of
G
by
\Delta_{i}^{j}
union
\sigma(c)
, for any cell
c\in C(\rho)
such that
E(\sigma(c))\cap E(P^{\prime}_{i}\cup P^{\prime}_{j})\neq\emptyset
.
Since
\mathcal{C}
is
R
-consistent, observe that what we are looking for is a transaction
\mathcal{Q}
such that, if
i
is the minimum index among all paths in
\mathcal{Q}
while
j
is the maximum, then
H_{i}^{j}\cap R=\emptyset
.

For
i\in[r(r-1)]
, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\{P_{(i-1)\cdot p+1},\ldots,P_{i\cdot p}\}
, where each
|\mathcal{P}_{i}|=p
.
Now we are in one of two cases.
Either there exists
i\in[r(r-1)]
, such that
H_{(i-1)\cdot p+1}^{i\cdot p}
contains no vertex of
R
, or all do.
In the former case we are done with
\mathcal{P}_{i}
being the desired transaction.
So we may assume we are in the latter.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be defined by collecting the first path from every
\mathcal{P}_{i},

i\in[r(r-1)]
.
Now observe that since
\mathcal{Q}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and
|\mathcal{C}|=r+1
,
|\mathcal{Q}|=r(r-1)
, there exists a
(2(r+1)\times r(r-1))
-mesh
M\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{C}\cup\bigcup\mathcal{Q}
such that
\bigcup\mathcal{Q}\subseteq M
.
Moreover, since for every
i\in[r(r-1)-1]
,
H_{(i-1)\cdot p+1}^{i\cdot p}
contains a red vertex, we infer that the subgraph of
G
induced by the
(r+1,i)
-brick of
M
contains a vertex of
R
.
Then, by calling
??
, we may conclude with an
r
-mesh
M^{\prime}\subseteq G
such that
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}
is a truncation of the tangle induced by an
r
-submesh
M^{\prime\prime}
of
M
whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

4.3
Refining nest trees

Splitting leaf nests.

We now have all the tools at our disposal to start splitting the nests of leaf societies for a given nest tree.
We first introduce some additional definitions to ease writing.

Let
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with a nest
\mathcal{C}
.
Moreover, let
\Delta
be the disk of the inner cycle of
\mathcal{C}
in
\rho
and
\mathcal{P}
be a
\rho
-flat,
\rho
-exposed transaction in
(G,\Omega)
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
.

Now, notice that since
\mathcal{P}
is
\rho
-exposed and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
, if we remove from
\Delta
the interior of the
\rho
-container of
\mathcal{P}
, we obtain precisely two
\rho
-aligned disks
\Delta^{*}_{1}
and
\Delta^{*}_{2}
which contain precisely the cells of
\rho
contained in
\Delta
and not in the
\rho
-container of
\mathcal{P}
.
We call
\Delta^{*}_{1}
and
\Delta^{*}_{2}
the
residual vortices
of
\rho
and
\mathcal{P}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

For all integers
s_{0},s,t,\ell\geq 1
with
s_{0}\geq t
, let
p=2t+2s_{0}+2s+6
.
Then the following holds.

Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with an
R
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}
of cycle order
2s_{0}+s+2
, linkage order
t
, reserve
s_{0}
, and
\ell
leaves.
Also, let
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
be a leaf society of
\mathfrak{T}
,
\rho^{\prime}
be the restriction of
\rho
to
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
, and
\mathcal{C}
be the nest of
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
in
\rho^{\prime}
.

Further, let
\mathcal{P}
be a
\rho^{\prime}
-flat,
\rho^{\prime}
-exposed transaction in
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
of order
p
that is
R
-blank and orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
and let
\Delta^{*}_{1}
and
\Delta^{*}_{2}
be the residual vortices of
\rho^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{P}
.

Now, assume that either
\rho^{\prime}
has a vortex, or that
V(G^{\prime})\cap R\neq\emptyset
.
Then,

i)

if for each
i\in[2]
, either
\Delta^{*}_{i}
contains a vortex, or there is a vertex of
R
in the crop of
G^{\prime}
by
\Delta^{*}_{i}
, then there exists an
R
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
of
(G,\Omega)
of cycle order
s
, linkage order
t
, reserve
s_{0}
, and
\ell+1
leaves, that respects
\mathfrak{T}
.

ii)

Otherwise, there exists a strictly tighter
R
-consistent nest tree of
(G,\Omega)
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds one of the two outcomes in time
\mathbf{poly}(t+s+s_{0})\cdot|V(G)||E(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
l
be the leaf node of
\mathfrak{T}
corresponding to
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
.
Also, assume that
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{2s_{0}+s+2}\}
, let
\mathcal{R}
be the radial linkage for
l
in
\mathfrak{T}
, and let
\Delta
be the disk of the boundary cycle for
l
in which the rendition
\rho^{\prime}
lives in.

Step 1: Setup.
Consider an ordering
P_{1},\ldots,P_{2t+2s_{0}+2s+4}
of
\mathcal{P}
so that it is indexed naturally.
With this, we split
\mathcal{P}
in
4
subtransactions to utilize in the construction steps that follow.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{2},\ldots,P_{t+1}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{t+2},\ldots,P_{t+s_{0}+s+3}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{t+s_{0}+s+4},\ldots,P_{t+2s_{0}+2s+5}\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{P_{t+2s_{0}+2s+6},\ldots,P_{2t+2s_{0}+2s+6}\}.

Notice that, with
\mathcal{P}
being
R
-blank, each of the
4
transactions above is also
R
-blank.

Next we define two disks
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
as follows.
Let
U_{1}
denote the cycle consisting of a subpath of
C_{s_{0}+s+2}
together with a subpath of
P_{t+s_{0}+s+3}
such that the
U_{1}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
avoids the paths in
\mathcal{S}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
Similarly, let
U_{2}
denote the cycle consisting of a subpath of
C_{s_{0}+s+2}
together with a subpath of
P_{t+s_{0}+s+4}
such that the
U_{2}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
avoids the paths in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{1}
.
Then, for each
i\in[2]
, we define
\Delta_{i}
to be the
U_{i}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
.

The two disks
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are our candidates for the new leaf societies.
Notice that for each
i\in[2]
,
\Delta^{*}_{i}\subseteq\Delta_{i}
.
Also note that at this point each
\Delta_{i}
also contains the infrastracture that we will utilize to define the reserved nests that surround the new leaf societies.

Step 2: Deciding the outcome.
Recall that
\mathcal{P}
is
\rho^{\prime}
-flat and
R
-blank.
This implies that every vortex of
\rho
is contained in either
\Delta^{*}_{1}
or
\Delta^{*}_{2}
.
Moreover, every vertex of
R
is a vertex of the union of the crop of
G^{\prime}
by
\Delta^{*}_{1}
and the crop of
G^{\prime}
by
\Delta^{*}_{2}
.
For each
i\in[2]
, we say that
\Delta^{*}_{i}
is
promising
if either
\Delta^{*}_{i}
contains a vortex of
\rho
or there is a vertex of
R
that is a vertex of the crop of
G^{\prime}
by
\Delta^{*}_{i}
.

Now by assumption, either
\rho^{\prime}
has a vortex or
V(G^{\prime})\cap R\neq\emptyset
.
Therefore, there is
i\in[2]
that is promising.
If both are promising we proceed with
Step 3a
.
Otherwise we proceed with
Step 3b
.

Step 3a: The split.
To ensure that the two new leaf societies we wish to produce are connected to the nest in reserve for
l
, we shall redefine it utilizing the outermost cycles of
\mathcal{C}
and the paths in
\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{l}=\{C_{s_{0}+s+3},\ldots,C_{2s_{0}+s+2}\}
,
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{l}
be the
C_{2s_{0}+s+2}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
, and
\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}
be the
C_{s_{0}+s+3}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
.
Note that
|\mathcal{C}_{l}|=s_{0}
.
Moreover, we define
\mathcal{R}_{l}
by cropping each path
R\in\mathcal{R}
to the minimal subpath with one endpoint in
V(\Omega_{\Delta^{\mathsf{in}}_{l}})
and the other being the endpoint of
R
not drawn in
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{l}
.

We now fix the following radial linkages which will be used to append the new leaf societies to
\mathfrak{T}
.
For every
T\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}
let
T^{\prime}
be a minimal
V(\Omega_{\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}_{l}})
-
(V(C_{1})\cap N(\rho^{\prime}))
-subpath of
T
.
We then set
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq\{T^{\prime}\mid T\in\mathcal{T}_{1}\}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq\{T^{\prime}\mid T\in\mathcal{T}_{2}\}
.

Next, we create new nests for each of the future leaf societies arising from
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
.
For each
j\in[s_{0}+s+1]
, let
C^{1}_{j}
be the cycle contained in
C_{j}\cup P_{t+1+j}
which is fully drawn within
\Delta_{1}
and such that the
C^{1}_{j}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
contains
\Delta^{*}_{1}
.
Then, set

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\coloneqq\{C^{1}_{j}\mid j\in[s_{0}+s+1]\}.

Similarly, for each
j\in[s_{0}+s+1]
, let
C^{2}_{j}
be the cycle contained in
C_{j}\cup P_{t+s_{0}+s+3+j}
which is fully drawn within
\Delta_{2}
and such that the
C^{2}_{j}
-disk in
\rho^{\prime}
contains
\Delta^{*}_{2}
.
Then, set

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq\{C^{2}_{j}\mid j\in[s_{0}+s+1]\}.

Also note that by construction for both
i\in[2]
,
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}
as well as to
\mathcal{C}_{l}
.

It remains to discuss how we obtain the desired nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.
First, we append to
\mathfrak{T}
two new leaf nodes, say
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
as the children of
l
.
For
l
, we discard the nest in reserve for

as well as the boundary cycle for
l
and place
\mathcal{C}_{l}
as the nest for
l
instead.
We moreover replace the radial linkage
\mathcal{R}
by
\mathcal{R}_{l}
.
Now, for each
i\in[2]
, we associate
l_{i}
with the nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}
.
The outermost
s_{0}
cycles serve as nest in reserve for
l_{i}
, the cycle
C^{i}_{s+1}
as the boundary cycle for
l_{i}
, and the rest as the leaf nest for
l_{i}
.
Moreover, we set
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}_{i}
to be the radial linkage associated to the edge
ll_{i}
of (the underlying tree of)
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.
Finally, it follows by construction that all required properties holds, especially that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
remains
R
-consistent.

Step 3b: Getting a strictly tighter nest tree.
We may now assume that exactly one of
\Delta^{*}_{1}
or
\Delta^{*}_{2}
is not promising.
Without loss of generality we may assume that
\Delta^{*}_{2}
is not promising.

Now, let
C_{2s_{0}+s+3}
be the boundary cycle for
l
and consider
\mathcal{C}^{*}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{2s_{0}+s+3}\}
.
Also, let
\Delta^{*}
be the
C_{2s_{0}+s+2}
-disk in
\rho
.

Now, let
\mathcal{T}^{*}
be defined by cropping each path
T\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
to a minimal
V(\Omega^{\prime})
-
(V(C_{1})\cap N(\rho))
-subpath of
T
, and let
\mathcal{R}^{*}
be the
\Delta
-truncation in
\rho
of
\mathcal{R}
.
Note that both
\mathcal{T}^{*}
and
\mathcal{R}^{*}
are radial linkages orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{*}
.

We may now apply
??
, which is possible since
|\mathcal{C}^{*}|\geq t+3,
in order to obtain a radial linkage
\mathcal{L}
for
\mathcal{C}^{*}
with the additional property that every edge of a path in
\mathcal{L}
drawn in the exterior of
\Delta^{*}
is an edge of a path in
\mathcal{R}^{*}
.
In particular, the endpoints of
\mathcal{L}
on
V(\Omega^{\prime})
are exactly those of
\mathcal{R}^{*}
.
This allows us to extend each path in
\mathcal{L}
through the non-truncated version of the corresponding path in
\mathcal{R}^{*}
, so as to reach their original endpoint drawn outside of
\Delta^{*}
,
thereby obtaining the radial linkage
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
.

The next step is to define the new tighter nest.
This construction is similar to before.
For each
i\in[2s_{0}+s+3]
, let
C^{\prime}_{i}
be the cycle in
C_{i}\cup P_{t+1+i}
such that the
C^{\prime}_{i}
-disk in
\rho
contains
\Delta^{*}_{1}
and avoids the drawing of every path in
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
Moreover, set
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\{C^{\prime}_{i}\mid i\in[2s_{0}+s+3]\}
.

Clearly, if
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}
is the
C^{\prime}_{2s_{0}+s+3}
-disk in
\rho
that contains
\Delta^{*}_{1}
, the crop of
G
by
\Delta^{\mathsf{out}}
is a proper subgraph of
G^{\prime}
, as no path in
\mathcal{T}_{2}
is contained in it.

Now, by applying
??
, we may assume (up to finding an even tighter nest than
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
) that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.
By replacing
\mathcal{C}^{*}
with
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{R}
with
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{T}
we obtain an
R
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
that is strictly tighter, thereby concluding the proof.
∎

Obtaining the final nest tree.

We are now in the position to show a crucial lemma towards the proof of
??
, which will inductively build a nest tree that either has a target number of leaves, or certifies that all leaf societies have bounded depth.

Lemma 4.14
.

There exist polynomial functions
\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{6}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all integers
s\geq t\geq r+1\geq 4
,
b\geq 0
,
d\geq 2
, and
\ell\geq 1
the following holds.

Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk with breadth
b
and depth
d
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a nest in
\rho
of order
\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}(s,\ell)
that is
R
-consistent.
Further, let
\mathcal{R}
be a radial linkage in
\rho
for
\mathcal{C}
of order
t
that is orthogonal to
\mathcal{C}
.

Then, there exists either

i)

an
r
-mesh
M\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho
and such that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is a truncation of the tangle induced by a mesh whose horizontal paths are subpaths of distinct cycles from
\mathcal{C}
, or

ii)

an
R
-consistent nest tree
\mathfrak{T}
in
\rho
with linkage order
t
and reserve
s
such that, either

•

\mathfrak{T}
has
\ell+1
leaves, or

•

at most
\ell
leaves and every leaf society of
\mathfrak{T}
has depth at most
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}(b,d,r,s,t,\ell)
.

Further, the root nest
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{T}
is a subset of
\mathcal{C}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mathfrak{T}
in time
\mathbf{poly}(b+d+s+\ell)\cdot|E(G)|^{3}.

Before we begin with the proof of
??
, we fix a recursive function
\mathsf{nestrec}
that represents the recursive application of
??
and ensures that the final nest tree we obtain has reserve
s_{0}
.
Let
s_{0}\geq t\geq r+1\geq 4
and
\zeta\geq 1
be integers. We define,

\displaystyle\mathsf{nestrec}(s_{0},0)

\displaystyle\coloneq 2

\displaystyle\mathsf{nestrec}(s_{0},\zeta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2s_{0}+2+\mathsf{nestrec}(s_{0},\zeta-1).

Unfolding the recursion we infer that

\mathsf{nestrec}(s_{0},\zeta)=2+2\zeta(s_{0}+1).

Moreover, assuming we start with a rendition in the disk of breadth
b\geq 1
and depth
d\geq 2
, the following value
p^{*}
is an estimate on the depth of the leaf societies.

p^{*}<(b+1)(2bd+12r^{2}s_{0}(t+(\zeta+1)s_{0}).

Based on these estimates let us define the the two functions
\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}
and
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}
, assuming
\ell+1\geq 2
is the goal number of leaves in the final nest tree.

\displaystyle\mathsf{nest}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}(s,\ell)

\displaystyle\coloneqq s+1+\mathsf{nestrec}(s,\ell)\in\mathbf{O}(\ell s),\ \text{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}(b,d,r,s,t,\ell)

\displaystyle\coloneqq p^{*}\in\mathbf{O}((b+1)(bd+r^{2}st+r^{2}s^{2}\ell)).

Proof of
??
.

We commence the proof with the construction of a first nest tree
\mathfrak{T}_{1}=(\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{1},T_{1},r,\phi^{1}_{1},\phi_{2}^{1})
in
(G,\Omega)
from
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{R})
with a single leaf as follows.
Let
(T_{1},r_{1})
be a graph with a single vertex
r_{1}
.
Then, let
\phi_{1}^{1}(r_{1})=\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}=\mathcal{R}
.

Let

Observe that

and

.
Hence

is a nest tree of

of cycle order

, linkage order

, with a single leaf.

We prove the following claim by iteratively applying

.
Consider an integer

.

Claim:

-mesh in

that is red in

.

Clearly, by iteratively applying the claim above, starting from

, such that

and

So consider an integer

and assume that we are given a nest tree

as above.

We begin by choosing some leaf society

of

to

Also, set

In what follows, whenever we encounter an outcome which produces a tighter nest tree, we mark it, and we assume that the other outcome is given.

If every leaf society has depth at most

we immediately conclude.
Therefore, we may assume that there exists a transaction

in

By
??
, we may assume that there is a transaction

Then, by

, we either find a

Now, we may call

Notice that by doing so we may have messed with the orthogonality of

This can be easily mended by calling

This gives us permission to call

Clearly

.
Now, by induction, since each subsequent nest tree we obtain respects the previous one, we know that the root nest of

is a subset of

Moreover, since

Therefore, we may always assume that we obtain the second outcome which is an

Note that, since this last step produces a strictly tighter nest tree, we are still making progress, even though we previously made the nest cozy.

It remains to discuss what we do in the case we produce a tighter nest tree.
Recall, that whenever this is the case, at least one edge of
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
is being “pushed” towards the “outside” of
(G^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime})
, and eventually fully outside the updated leaf society.
Since there are at most

edges and
s_{\zeta}
many cycles in
\mathcal{C}_{\zeta}
, we cannot find a tighter nest tree more that
s_{\zeta}|E(G^{\prime})|
times.
As a result, we may assume that we always end up in an outcome which does not produce a tighter nest tree and with this we may conclude.
∎

4.3.1
Obtaining the blank rendition

We finally have all the tools required to produce the desired blank rendition.
What we have to show is that, given a nest tree as in the outcome of
??
, we can produce the surface wall needed for outcome v) of
??
.

We prove two lemmas.
The first shows how starting from a grounded annulus wall in a rendition of a society, we can build a nest tree as in the outcome of
??
with large enough reserve, and then employing an argument using Menger’s Theorem, use the structure provided by the nest tree to link back the leaf societies, with their surrounding nest, to what is left of the annulus wall, thereby building the surface wall.

Lemma 4.15
.

There exist polynomial functions
\mathsf{wall}_{\ref{lemma:nestTreeToSurfaceWall}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{3}\to\mathbb{N}
and
\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:nestTreeToSurfaceWall}}\colon\mathbb{N}^{5}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for all integers
r\geq 3
,
b\geq 0
,

, and

the following holds.

Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society

in the disk with breadth

and depth

.

be a

-annulus wall that is grounded in

such that every vortex of
\rho
and every vertex of
R
is contained in the disk of the inner base cycle of

.

Then, there exists either

i)

an
r
-mesh

is a truncation of

-surface-wall

•

\displaystyle\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:nestTreeToSurfaceWall}}(r,k,\ell,b,d)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathsf{depth}_{\ref{lemma:finalNestTree}}(b,d,r,r+4k(\ell+2)^{2},r+4k(\ell+2)^{2},\ell).

Let

of all paths in the radial linkage of the root node, and let
Y\subseteq V(G^{\prime\prime})
be the set of all endpoints of the radial linkages of leaf nodes on the inner cycle of their corresponding leaf nest.
Note that
|X|=|Y|=4k\zeta(\zeta+1)
.

We now utilize Menger’s theorem in
G^{\prime\prime}
, asking for an
X
-
Y
linkage
\mathcal{P}
of order
4k\zeta(\zeta+1)
.
We claim that such a linkage exists.
Towards a contradiction, let us assume that this is not the case.
Then, there exists a set
Z\subseteq V(G^{\prime\prime})
with
|Z|<4k\zeta(\zeta+1)
that intersects every
X
-
Y
-path in
G^{\prime\prime}
.
First, let us observe that there exists at least one leaf node
l
of
\mathfrak{T}
for which, if
\mathcal{C}_{l}
and
\mathcal{R}_{l}
are the corresponding leaf nest and radial linkage, there is at least one cycle in
\mathcal{C}_{l}
and at least one path in
\mathcal{R}_{l}
that
Z
cannot intersect.
Indeed, let
r=t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,t_{\eta},t_{\eta+1}=l
be the root to leaf path reaching
l
in
\mathfrak{T}
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{r},\mathcal{R}_{r},\ldots,\mathcal{C}_{t_{i}},\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}},\ldots,\mathcal{R}_{t_{\eta-1}},\mathcal{C}_{l}
, be an alternating sequence of nest and radial linkages from
\mathfrak{T}
such that for
i\in[\eta]
,
\mathcal{C}_{t_{i}}
is the nest associated to
t_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
is the radial linkage associated to the edge
t_{i}t_{i+1}
in
\mathfrak{T}
.

Observe that, by definition of nest trees, for each choice of a cycle
C_{t_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}_{t_{i}}
and each choice of path
R_{t_{i}}\in\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
, the graph
\bigcup_{i}C_{t_{i}}\cup R_{t_{i}}
is connected.
Therefore, since
\mathfrak{T}
has linkage order and reserve at least
4k\zeta(\zeta+1)
, there is such a choice of cycles and paths that is disjoint from
Z
.
In this graph we can clearly find an
X
-
Y
-path, which is a contradiction, and therefore our claim holds.

Moreover, since we choose precisely
4k(\zeta+1)
paths from each leaf radial linkage, the
X
-
Y
-linkage we find is orthogonal to each leaf nest.
There is one more technical issue that we have to resolve.
Menger’s Theorem does not allow us to control exactly how the endpoints of these paths may be shuffled along
\Omega^{\prime}
.
However, by a simple pigeonhole argument, since
\mathfrak{T}
has
\zeta
leaves and we chose the numbers so that
\nicefrac{{|\mathcal{P}|}}{{(\zeta+1)}}=4k\zeta
, there exist disjoint segments
I_{1},\ldots,I_{\zeta}
of
\Omega^{\prime}
such that there is a subset
\mathcal{P}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
with
\mathcal{P}_{i}=4k
, of paths with one endpoint in
V(I_{i})
and the other in the inner cycle of the leaf nest for the
same
leaf node.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in\zeta}\mathcal{P}_{i}
.
We may now extend each path in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
via the vertical path of
W
with which it shares an endpoint in
V(\Omega^{\prime})
, all the way to its other endpoint in
V(C_{\mathsf{wall}_{\ref{lemma:nestTreeToSurfaceWall}}(r,k,\ell,b,d)})
.
With this step we have managed to attach all leaf nests onto the remainder of
W
which gives us the desired extended
k
-surface-wall
D
with signature
(0,0,\zeta)
.
By the assumptions on
\mathfrak{T}
, all desired properties for
D
follow directly.
∎

The next and final lemma shows that if we obtain an extended surface wall as above with a sufficient number of vortex segments we may still conclude with a red mesh.

Lemma 4.16
.

For every integer
r\geq 3
the following holds.
Let
(G,R)
be an annotated graph and
\rho
be a rendition of a society
(G,\Omega)
in the disk.
Further, let
D\subseteq G
be an extended
(r(r-1))
-surface-wall grounded in
\rho
with signature
(0,0,r(r-1)-1)
such that, for every vortex segment
S
of
D
there is a
\rho
-aligned disk
\Delta_{S}
contained in the disk bounded by the trace of the inner cycle of
S
that avoids the trace of the simple cycle of
D
and there is a vertex of
R
that is a vertex of the crop of
G
by
\Delta_{S}
.
Then, there exists an
r
-mesh
M\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho
and such that
\mathcal{T}_{M}
is a truncation of
\mathcal{T}_{D}
.

Moreover, there exists an algorithm that finds
\mu
in time
\mathbf{poly}(r)\cdot|E(G)|
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{r(r-1)}\}
be the base cycles of
D
ordered from innermost to outermost and
\mathcal{P}=\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{4br(r-1)}\}
be the vertical paths of
D
ordered from left to right so that
P_{1},\ldots,P_{2r(r-1)}
and
P_{4br(r-1)-2r(r-1)+1},\ldots,P_{4br(r-1)}
are the vertical paths of the wall segment of
D
.
With respect to this ordering, for every vortex segment
S
of
D
, let
\mathcal{C}_{S}=\{C^{S}_{1},\ldots,C^{S}_{r(r-1)}\}
be the nest of
S
ordered from outermost to innermost and
\mathcal{R}_{S}=\{R^{S}_{1},\ldots,R^{S}_{4r(r-1)}\}
be the rails of
S
ordered left to right.
Moreover, if
S_{i}
is the
i
-th vortex segment from left to right in this ordering, let
\mathcal{P}_{S_{i}}
be the subset of
\mathcal{P}
that are vertical paths of
S_{i}
ordered from left to right as well.

We first explain how to obtain an
(2(r+1)\times r(r-1))
-mesh
M\subseteq
such that for every
j\in[r(r-1)-1]
the subgraph of
G
induced by the
(r+1,j)
-brick of
M
contains a vertex of
R
.
We do so by explaining how to define its
2(r+1)
horizontal paths
T_{1},\ldots,T_{2(r+1)}
and its
r(r-1)
vertical paths
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{r(r-1)}
.

We define the
i
-th horizontal path
T_{i}
as
P_{i}
when
i\leq r+1
and as
P_{4br(r-1)r+i}
otherwise.
Moreover, we define the
r(r-1)
-th vertical path
Q_{r(r-1)}
as the subpath of
C_{r(r-1)}
with endpoints on
T_{1}
and
T_{2r}
that intersects all
T_{i}
’s.

For
i\in[r(r-1)-1]
, we define the
i
-th vertical path as follows.
We start from a vertex of
T_{i}
on
C_{i}
, and move towards the right, until we hit the
i
-th vertical path
P^{S_{i}}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{S_{i}}
.
From there we move upwards along
P^{S_{i}}_{i}
, continuing on
R^{S_{i}}_{i}\in\mathcal{R}_{S_{i}}
, until we hit the
i
-th cycle
C^{S_{i}}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{S_{i}}
.
Then we continue on
C^{S_{i}}_{i}
until we reach
R^{S_{i}}_{4r(r-1)-i+1}\in\mathcal{R}_{S_{i}}
, by following the subpath of
C^{S_{i}}_{i}
that does not intersect any
R^{S_{i}}_{j}
, where
i<j<4r(r-1)-i+1
.
From there, we continue downwards, onto the
4r(r-1)-i+1
-th vertical path
P^{S_{i}}_{4r(r-1)-i+1}\in\mathcal{P}_{S_{i}}
, until we reach
C_{i}
once more.
We repeat the above and visit all vortex segments to the right of
S_{i}
in precisely the same way.
Finally, we continue along
C_{i}
towards the right, until we intersect
T_{2(r+1)}
, where we stop.

It is not hard to see that
M
is indeed the desired mesh.
See
??
for an illustration of the above procedure.
In particular we may observe that, the subgraph induced by the
(r+1,j)
-brick of
M
,
j\in r(r-1)-1
, contains the crop of
G
by
\Delta_{S_{i}}
and therefore at least one vertex of
R
.
We may now conclude by applying
??
which gives us the desired
r
-mesh
M^{\prime}\subseteq G
that is grounded and red in
\rho
.
Clearly
\mathcal{T}_{M^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{D}
.
∎
